Abstract
Report issue for preceding element
In the wake of recent progress on quantum computing hardware, the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) is standardizing cryptographic protocols that are resistant to attacks by quantum adversaries. The primary digital signature scheme that NIST has chosen is 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲  -  𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲 - 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{CRYSTALS}\raisebox{1.0pt}{-}\mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_CRYSTALS - sansserif_Dilithium . The hardness of this scheme is based on the hardness of three computational problems: Module Learning with Errors ( 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE ), Module Short Integer Solution ( 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS ), and 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS . 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE and 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS have been well-studied and are widely believed to be secure. However, 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS is novel and, though classically as hard as 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS , its quantum hardness is unclear. In this paper, we provide the first proof of the hardness of 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS via a reduction from 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE in the Quantum Random Oracle Model (QROM). Our proof uses recently developed techniques in quantum reprogramming and rewinding. A central part of our approach is a proof that a certain hash function, derived from the 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS problem, is collapsing. From this approach, we deduce a new security proof for 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium under appropriate parameter settings. Compared to the only other rigorous security proof for a variant of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium , 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆  -  𝖰𝖱𝖮𝖬 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 - 𝖰𝖱𝖮𝖬 \mathsf{Dilithium}\raisebox{1.0pt}{-}\mathsf{QROM} sansserif_Dilithium - sansserif_QROM , our proof has the advantage of being applicable under the condition q = 1  mod  2  n 𝑞 1 mod 2 𝑛 q=1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2n italic_q = 1 roman_mod 2 italic_n , where q 𝑞 q italic_q denotes the modulus and n 𝑛 n italic_n the dimension of the underlying algebraic ring. This condition is part of the original 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium proposal and is crucial for the efficient implementation of the scheme. We provide new secure parameter sets for 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium under the condition q = 1  mod  2  n 𝑞 1 mod 2 𝑛 q=1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2n italic_q = 1 roman_mod 2 italic_n , finding that our public key sizes and signature sizes are about 2.5 × 2.5\times 2.5 × to 2.8 × 2.8\times 2.8 × larger than those of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆  -  𝖰𝖱𝖮𝖬 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 - 𝖰𝖱𝖮𝖬 \mathsf{Dilithium}\raisebox{1.0pt}{-}\mathsf{QROM} sansserif_Dilithium - sansserif_QROM for the same security levels.

1 Introduction
Report issue for preceding element
Quantum computers are theoretically capable of breaking the underlying computational hardness assumptions for many existing cryptographic schemes. Therefore, it is vitally important to develop new cryptographic primitives and protocols that are resistant to quantum attacks. The goal of NIST's Post-Quantum Cryptography Standardization Project is to design a new generation of cryptographic schemes that are secure against quantum adversaries. In 2022, NIST selected three new digital signature schemes for standardization [ Ala+22] : Falcon, SPHINCS+, and 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲  -  𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲 - 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{CRYSTALS}\raisebox{1.0pt}{-}\mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_CRYSTALS - sansserif_Dilithium . Of the three, 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲  -  𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖢𝖱𝖸𝖲𝖳𝖠𝖫𝖲 - 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{CRYSTALS}\raisebox{1.0pt}{-}\mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_CRYSTALS - sansserif_Dilithium [ Bai+21] , or 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium in shorthand, was identified as the primary choice for post-quantum digital signing.
Report issue for preceding element
To practically implement post-quantum cryptography, users must be provided with not only assurance that a scheme is secure in a post-quantum setting, but also the means by which to judge parameter choices and thereby balance their own needs for security and efficiency. The goal of the current work is to provide rigorous assurance of the security of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium as well as implementable parameter sets. A common model for the security of digital signatures is existential unforgeability against chosen message attacks, or 𝖤𝖴𝖥  -  𝖢𝖬𝖠 𝖤𝖴𝖥 - 𝖢𝖬𝖠 \mathsf{EUF}\raisebox{0.0pt}{-}\mathsf{CMA} sansserif_EUF - sansserif_CMA . In this setting, an adversary is allowed to make sequential queries to a signing oracle for the signature scheme, and then afterwards the adversary attempts to forge a signature for a new message. We work in the setting of strong existential unforgeability ( 𝗌𝖤𝖴𝖥  -  𝖢𝖬𝖠 𝗌𝖤𝖴𝖥 - 𝖢𝖬𝖠 \mathsf{sEUF}\raisebox{0.0pt}{-}\mathsf{CMA} sansserif_sEUF - sansserif_CMA ) wherein we must also guard against the possibility that an adversary could try to forge a new signature for one of the messages already signed by the oracle. (See Section 2 for details.)
Report issue for preceding element
Additionally, we utilize the quantum random oracle model (QROM) for hash functions. We recall that when a hash function H : X → Y : 𝐻 → 𝑋 𝑌 H:X\rightarrow Y italic_H : italic_X → italic_Y is used as a subroutine in a digital signature scheme, the random oracle model (ROM) assumes that one can replace each instance of the function H 𝐻 H italic_H with a black box that accepts inputs from X 𝑋 X italic_X and returns outputs in Y 𝑌 Y italic_Y according to a uniformly randomly chosen function from X 𝑋 X italic_X to Y 𝑌 Y italic_Y . (This model is useful because random functions are easier to work with in theory than actual hash functions.) The random oracle model needs to be refined in the quantum setting because queries to the hash function can be made in superposition: for any quantum state of the form ∑ x ∈ X α x  | x ⟩ subscript 𝑥 𝑋 subscript 𝛼 𝑥 ket 𝑥 \sum_{x\in X}\alpha_{x}\left|x\right> ∑ start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x ∈ italic_X end_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_α start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x end_POSTSUBSCRIPT | italic_x ⟩ , where ∀ x ∈ X , α x ∈ ℂ formulae-sequence for-all 𝑥 𝑋 subscript 𝛼 𝑥 ℂ \forall x\in X,\alpha_{x}\in\mathbb{C} ∀ italic_x ∈ italic_X , italic_α start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ∈ blackboard_C , a quantum computer can efficiently prepare the superposed state ∑ x ∈ X α x  | x ⟩  | H  ( x ) ⟩ subscript 𝑥 𝑋 subscript 𝛼 𝑥 ket 𝑥 ket 𝐻 𝑥 \sum_{x\in X}\alpha_{x}\left|x\right>\left|H(x)\right> ∑ start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x ∈ italic_X end_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_α start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x end_POSTSUBSCRIPT | italic_x ⟩ | italic_H ( italic_x ) ⟩ . The quantum random oracle model (QROM) therefore assumes that each use of the hash function can be simulated by a black box that accepts a quantum state supported on X 𝑋 X italic_X and returns a quantum state supported on X × Y 𝑋 𝑌 X\times Y italic_X × italic_Y (computed by a truly random function from X 𝑋 X italic_X to Y 𝑌 Y italic_Y ) [ Bon+11] . While no efficient and truly random functions actually exist, the QROM is generally trusted and it enables the application of a number of useful proof techniques.

Eq. 4 implies that breaking the 𝗌𝖤𝖴𝖥  -  𝖢𝖬𝖠 𝗌𝖤𝖴𝖥 - 𝖢𝖬𝖠 \mathsf{sEUF}\raisebox{0.0pt}{-}\mathsf{CMA} sansserif_sEUF - sansserif_CMA security of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium is at least as hard as solving one of the 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE , 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS , or 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS problems. While 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE and 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS are known to be no harder than 𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{LWE} sansserif_LWE and 𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SIS} sansserif_SIS , respectively, there are no known attacks taking advantage of their module structure so it is generally believed that they are as hard as their unstructured counterparts [ LS15] . In turn, 𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{LWE} sansserif_LWE and 𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SIS} sansserif_SIS are at least as hard as the (Gap) Shortest Vector Problem, which is the underlying hard problem of lattice cryptography [ Ajt96, Reg09, Pei16] .

However, the final problem, 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS , is novel and so its difficulty is an open question. The problem is known to be as classically hard as 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS since there exists a reduction from 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS to 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS in the ROM [ KLS18, BN06] . The reduction uses the following “rewinding” argument. Any randomized algorithm can be specified by a deterministic circuit with auxiliary random bits. Therefore, given a randomized algorithm for 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS , we can run its deterministic circuit with some randomly chosen bits to obtain one solution and then rewind and run it again using the same bits chosen from before, while at the same time reprogramming the random oracle at the query corresponding to the output of the first run, to obtain a second solution. Subtracting these two solutions to 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS yields a solution to 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS . However, the argument fails for the following reasons in the QROM (where a quantum algorithm can make queries in superposition to a quantum random oracle):
Report issue for preceding element
The randomness in a quantum algorithm includes the randomness of measurement outcomes. We cannot run a quantum algorithm twice and guarantee that the “random bits” will be the same in both runs because we cannot control measurement outcomes. More generally, we cannot rewind a quantum algorithm to a post-measurement state. Report issue for preceding element
Since a quantum algorithm can make queries in superposition, it is no longer clear where to reprogram the random oracle. Report issue for preceding element
Currently, the only explicit rigorous proof of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium 's security based on conventional hardness assumptions [ KLS18] requires modifying the parameters such that q = 5  mod  8 𝑞 5 mod 8 q=5,\text{mod},8 italic_q = 5 mod 8 and 2  γ < q / 2 2 𝛾 𝑞 2 2\gamma<\sqrt{q/2} 2 italic_γ < square-root start_ARG italic_q / 2 end_ARG , where γ 𝛾 \gamma italic_γ is a length upper bound on vectors corresponding to valid signatures. 1.2 Overview of main result
Report issue for preceding element
The main result of our paper is the first proof of the computational hardness of the 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS problem, presented in Section 3. This hardness result implies a new security proof for 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium which, unlike the previous proof in [ KLS18] , applies to the case q = 1  mod  2  n 𝑞 1 mod 2 𝑛 q=1,\text{mod},2n italic_q = 1 mod 2 italic_n . Specifically, we reduce 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE to 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS . By Eq. 4, our result implies that the security of 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium (with parameters that are not too far from the original parameters) can be based on the hardness of 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE and 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{MSIS} sansserif_MSIS .

We now give a high-level overview of the proof. The first step is to define two experiments: the chosen-coordinate binding experiment 𝖢𝖢𝖡 𝖢𝖢𝖡 \mathsf{CCB} sansserif_CCB and the collapsing experiment 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 \mathsf{Collapse} sansserif_Collapse . These experiments are interactive protocols between a verifier and a prover. The protocols end with the verifier outputting a bit b 𝑏 b italic_b . If b = 1 𝑏 1 b=1 italic_b = 1 , the prover is said to win the experiment. The reduction then proceeds in three steps: (i) reduce winning 𝖢𝖢𝖡 𝖢𝖢𝖡 \mathsf{CCB} sansserif_CCB to solving 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS , (ii) reduce winning 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 \mathsf{Collapse} sansserif_Collapse to winning 𝖢𝖢𝖡 𝖢𝖢𝖡 \mathsf{CCB} sansserif_CCB , and (iii) reduce solving 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 𝖬𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{MLWE} sansserif_MLWE to winning 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅𝖺𝗉𝗌𝖾 \mathsf{Collapse} sansserif_Collapse . In this paper, we use the definition of the 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 𝖣𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗍𝗁𝗂𝗎𝗆 \mathsf{Dilithium} sansserif_Dilithium signature scheme as specified in [ Bai+21] . We now give the formal definitions of the chosen-coordinate binding and collapsing experiments mentioned in the introduction. More general versions of these definitions can be found in, e.g., [ Unr12, DS23] . First, we define a “plain” version of 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿𝖳𝖺𝗋𝗀𝖾𝗍𝖬𝖲𝖨𝖲 \mathsf{SelfTargetMSIS} sansserif_SelfTargetMSIS , where the input matrix is not given in Hermite Normal Form.
